| G128 | **Being somewhere is an experience Cicada cannot capture — visits, places and what actually held your attention there** (Rodrigo 2026-09-03, forwarding [MemPal (arXiv:2502.01801)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.01801): "might be relevant for spatial memory understanding, and research data capture from video, so if I go to a museum, what are the artworks that influence me most") | **The gap:** every capture channel today is a screen — conversations, links, bookmarks, feeds. A morning in a museum, a walk, a conference floor leaves *nothing* in the bank unless the person types about it afterwards, so the stream (Era of Experience) has a hole exactly where lived experience is richest. `location` is already an entity type but nothing writes one from being there. **What the paper actually shows, and the ruling it suggests:** MemPal (Maniar, Chan, Zulfikar, Ren, Xu, Maes; MIT Media Lab, Feb 2025) puts a wearable camera on 15 older adults in their own homes, runs the egocentric video through a multimodal LLM, and keeps **an automated text diary of activities** — not a 3D map, not stored frames — then answers "where did I leave X?" by voice; object-finding measurably improved against no aid. The transferable result is the storage choice: **a text diary indexed by time and context was enough for spatial recall**. So Cicada should not build a spatial index or a scene graph; a *visit* is an episode with a place, and the things in it are ordinary entities with claims. Video, if it is ever used, is a **transducer to text at the edge and is then discarded** — the same shape as G105's block-level rule and G94's aggregates-never-samples rule. **Three tiers, cheapest first, each shippable alone:** **(a) Visits from what you already capture — zero new hardware, no camera, do this first.** The camera roll already answers "where was I and what struck me": photos carry EXIF time + GPS, and *the act of photographing is the attention signal* — in a museum you photograph the works that hold you. A `visit` episode is built by clustering a day's photos by place and time (Apple Photos / an exported album, the same import shape as the browser channels: the app reads, the backend parses), with the place resolved to a `location` entity; each photo becomes a media item on that visit, captions and any voice memo attached, and nothing is uploaded anywhere. Sleep then extracts the works/people/ideas named in captions or recognised text through the ordinary Stage-1 → Stage-2 path. **The camera roll is also the first step toward spatial understanding, and it is worth saying why: the photo is already the index.** Time and GPS put you in a place; what is *in* the frame says what held you there. Run that extraction **on-device in the app** (which already holds the file-access half of the division: the app reads `~/Pictures`/PhotoKit, the backend parses text) using Apple's Vision framework, in this order of yield: **(i) text (`VNRecognizeTextRequest`) — the highest-value and least speculative.** In a museum the wall label *is* the metadata: artist, title, year, medium, and people photograph the placard beside the work. OCR of that placard turns a JPEG into a real `media`/`concept` entity with an artist `person` beside it, rather than a filename. The same request covers signs, book covers, conference slides, menus and receipts, so it pays off far beyond galleries. **(ii) scene and object classification (`VNClassifyImageRequest`)** for a coarse, honest label (painting · sculpture · street · food · whiteboard) — enough to say *what kind of thing* a visit was made of, never asserted as fact beyond a confidence. **(iii) image feature prints (`VNGenerateImageFeaturePrintRequest`)** to collapse the eleven near-identical shots of one painting into one item, and to notice the same work photographed again years later — which is recurrence (G120) measured on pixels rather than words. **(iv) faces: detect, never identify.** `VNDetectFaceRectanglesRequest` can say a photo has people in it; naming them is the owner's act, not the machine's — a name arrives only by the owner tagging it, and it resolves against contacts (G81). Strangers in the background of a gallery shot are never clustered, never named, never stored as a face. **What Apple/Google do that cannot simply be borrowed, stated so nobody plans on it:** PhotoKit exposes **no public People API** (the People album's face clusters and names are Apple's, not a third party's to read), and **Visual Look Up** — the feature that names an artwork or a landmark from the photo itself — is not available to third-party apps. So the equivalent of "Apple knows this is a Rothko" is, for Cicada: the placard's OCR text, plus the owner confirming the guess through the ordinary inbox question. That is slower and more honest, and it produces a citable claim (G118) instead of an opaque label. Reverse-geocoding a coordinate to a venue name is a network call (`CLGeocoder`) and therefore opt-in per the connector rule, or skipped in favour of the coordinate plus the owner naming the place once. **What is stored:** the derived text and the asset's local identifier — never a copy of the image, never a face, never an upload. **(b) In-the-moment notes tied to a place.** Telegram `/note` exists; give it (and G91's share sheet) an optional place, so "this room stopped me" lands on the visit rather than floating free. **(c) Continuous or wearable video — RESEARCH, not a build.** Only after (a) and (b) prove the visit shape. Open questions the row exists to answer: what runs on-device (frame → text) so no frame is ever stored or sent; what cadence is enough (MemPal's diary is periodic, not per-frame); and the two hard constraints that are not technical — **bystander privacy** (a camera in a public gallery records other people, who have not consented and are not the owner's memory to keep; the answer is probably that only the owner's own utterances and derived descriptions survive, never faces, never a frame) and **venue rules** (many museums forbid recording; a channel that quietly breaks a venue's rules is not shippable). Also worth measuring before building: whether (a) alone answers the museum question well enough that (c) is not worth its cost. **"Which artworks influenced me most" is a query, not a capture feature.** Once visits exist it decomposes into three signals already designed: **attention frequency** (G120 — how often a work or artist recurs across visits, photos and later conversation), **provenance** (G118 — the evidence for "this moved me" is the caption you wrote or the sentence you said, cited as a span), and **decay** (G66 — a work you never mention again fades, and that silence is the honest answer to "influenced"; the ones that keep coming back are the ones that did). The strongest signal is not dwell time in the room, it is **re-mention weeks later** — which the graph already measures. **Rails:** no frame or raw sample enters a bank (G94); nothing is uploaded to a third party; place data is the owner's, so a visit is as private as an episode and never leaves the machine; bystanders are never identified; capture is opt-in per channel like every other. → extends **G94** (life-data streams — this is its place-and-time half), **G91** (share sheet as the in-the-moment path), **G77** (voice is the natural capture verb when your hands are busy looking); consumes **G120** (recurrence), **G118** (why you say it moved you), **G105**'s edge-extraction rule; feeds **G93** (a cross-stream question like this one is exactly what it is for). RESEARCH for (c), APPLY for (a)/(b), M | 🔲 |
| G129 | **Bookmarks as a live channel, both directions — a browser save shows up in the queue in seconds, an unbookmark asks before it forgets** (Rodrigo 2026-09-04: "as soon as i bookmark a website in chrome, does it get detected… i want it to be the most performant while also detecting the addition as soon as possible… if i remove a bookmark from chrome, it should appear in a sub section of deletions, but we need user confirmation to remove from memory (remove or keep)") | **Today the answer is no.** Bookmark capture is pull, not push, and the pull is a button: both call sites are user-pressed (`ChannelSourceView` "Sync now", the Feed strip's ⋯ action), there is no timer, no watcher, and the Sleep tail polls connectors, feeds and calendars but never bookmarks. Measured on the owner's machine 2026-09-04: `chrome-bookmarks connected=False count=0 last_sync=None` while Chrome's own file had been written that afternoon — a browser the product lists and had never once read. **(a) Mechanism — watch, don't poll, and a webhook does not exist.** Chrome has no local notification any third-party process can subscribe to; the only true push is a Chrome extension (`chrome.bookmarks.onCreated`), which costs an install, a permission prompt and a distribution story, and is rejected for now — revisit only if the watch proves unreliable. Polling re-reads and re-parses a 260 KB JSON on a timer to learn nothing, and still detects late. **A file watch is both the cheapest and the fastest**: Chrome rewrites its `Bookmarks` file on every change, so a `DispatchSource`/FSEvents watch costs nothing while idle and fires within a second or two of the save. **The trap to write down: Chrome replaces that file atomically** (write a temp, rename over it), so a vnode watch on the file's descriptor follows the dead inode and works exactly once — watch the containing directory, or re-arm on `.delete`/`.rename`. Debounce the burst, and skip the sync when size+mtime are unchanged, so a rewrite that changed nothing costs one `stat`. **(b) It lives in the app, and that bounds it honestly.** The launchd backend has no Full Disk Access and must never read `~/Library` (the R1 ruling behind the app-reads-bytes seam) — so the watcher is app-side, and detection only runs while the app runs. The catch-up half is a sync-if-changed pass on launch, which is also what finally reads a browser that has never been synced. **(c) Per-browser rows with a status light**, generalised over the existing `BrowserFile` enum rather than special-cased per browser: `never` · `watching` · `syncing` · `stale` (the file changed and we have not caught up) · `blocked` (needs Full Disk Access — the one state with a fix button, R9) · `absent` (browser or profile not installed) · `failed`. The light is a join: the backend owns `last_sync`/`last_error`/`last_skip` per channel in `sync_state.json`, the app owns whether the watch is armed. **(d) Deletions, which are the hard half.** Sync is additive today — `ingest_batch` dedups on url-hash and nothing records the set that was seen — so removal needs a previous set to diff against. Two rails make it correct rather than destructive: **the diff is only valid inside the scope that was synced** (with `folders:` selection, everything outside the chosen folders looks deleted — compute removals within the selected prefixes, or refuse to compute them at all), and **the diff is Chrome-then vs Chrome-now, never Chrome vs memory** — a URL the user chose to keep has already left Chrome, so a memory-based diff would re-propose it after every sync, forever. That means one small per-channel set of url-hashes beside `url_index.json`. **Removal is a proposal, never an act**: it writes an inbox item (the existing agent-proposes-user-disposes queue, `keep` / `remove`, resolved with `Cicada-Author: user`) and the browser's source page renders those same items as its own Deletions subsection — one write path, two views. `remove` archives the media entity rather than deleting the page: it is evergreen, may be claim-linked, and git keeps the history either way. A URL still reachable from another source (a manual save, another browser) is stated as such on the card, since the media entity is deduped by URL and its `origin` records only whoever got there first. **Rails:** the queue page needs no new plumbing — a sync writes episodes, the `episodes` sync component ticks, and SSE moves the Sleep queue on its own; nothing here reads a bank file from the backend; an unreadable file is one failure with the fix beside it, never a retry loop. → extends **G30** (bookmark ingestion), **G62** (channel state), **G124** (the Sources page these rows live on); generalises to **G119** (Arc/Brave/Firefox) for free, since the watch keys off `BrowserFile`; the deletions half is the first *retraction* signal in the product and pairs with **G66** decay and **G113** (a resolution is a reward signal). APPLY, M — **slice 1** = watch + catch-up + status light; **slice 2** = the seen-set, the removal proposal and the Deletions subsection. | 🛠️ **slice 1 shipped 2026-09-04 (PR #52)** — `BrowserWatch.swift`: one directory-level `DispatchSource` per browser (the atomic-replace trap, with a test that replaces a file three times and asserts all three are seen), 1.2 s debounce, a 5 s per-channel floor, size+mtime signature recorded only after a sync succeeds, catch-up on launch, and the six-state light on the Feed row and the source page. **Found on the owner's machine when it first ran:** the `chrome-bookmarks` row read `connected=False count=0` while 294 Chrome bookmark episodes sat in the bank from a July sync under the pre-split combined `bookmarks` key — the data was there and the row was blind. The catch-up ingested the 6 bookmarks saved since July that had never reached memory, and repaired the row. Verified live: a real change to the real file synced with no button pressed. Watching is bookmarks-only; iCloud tabs stay manual, because a tab opening is not a save. **Slice 2 (deletions) is next and not started.** |
| G130 | **App-wide zoom with ⌘+ / ⌘− — make the whole UI bigger or smaller, not just the graph canvas** (Rodrigo 2026-09-04: "using command + - i can modify the zoom of the UI") | **What exists today is the graph's zoom, not the app's.** `ZoomControls` in `ContentView.swift` drives `GraphViewModel.zoomAction` into the d3 transform inside the WKWebView — it scales *nodes and edges on the canvas* and nothing else. Every other surface (sidebar, Feed rows, Inbox cards, the Sleep queue, entity cards, Settings) renders at fixed sizes: `CicadaTheme` hardcodes `titleFont` 20 / `headingFont` 16 / `bodyFont` 13 / `captionFont` 11 and a fixed spacing scale, so there is no lever a keystroke could turn. **And the shortcut is not bound at all** — the app defines no `.commands` / `CommandGroup` anywhere, so it has no View menu and ⌘+/⌘− fall through to nothing; the eight existing `keyboardShortcut` calls are all view-local (⌘K, ⌘F, ⌘1–6, ⌘N, ⌘[). **(a) The lever should be one scale factor, not a font override.** A single persisted `uiScale` (`@AppStorage`, clamped, e.g. 0.8 – 1.6 in ~10% steps, 1.0 = today) that `CicadaTheme`'s font and spacing constants are *derived from* — which means those constants stop being `static let` and become reads through the same `@Observable` store the theme mode uses, so a change invalidates the views that read them. That is the PR #49 lesson repeated: a plain `static var` notifies nothing, and the setter must be idempotent or a write during render loops. **The `.id()` sledgehammer is forbidden here for the same reason it was removed in PR #49** — rebuilding the whole tree on a zoom step would tear down and reload the graph WKWebView on every keypress. **(b) The graph web view is the hard half, and it needs its own decision.** Scaling the SwiftUI chrome does not touch WKWebView content; the lever there is `pageZoom`, and it must not fight the d3 zoom transform the canvas already owns (a user who zoomed the graph to a neighbourhood must not have it silently re-fit). Cheapest honest answer for slice 1: **leave the graph canvas alone** — it has its own zoom controls and ⌘+/⌘− means *chrome*, not *canvas*, exactly as it does in a browser's own UI vs. a page's map widget. Revisit only if the graph's own labels read as mismatched beside scaled chrome. **(c) Bind it properly, not view-locally.** ⌘+/⌘−/⌘0 belong in a `CommandGroup(after: .sidebar)` (or `.toolbar`) View menu — `Zoom In` / `Zoom Out` / `Actual Size` — so the binding is discoverable in the menu bar, works from any tab, and does not collide with the graph's own controls. Note the keyboard reality: ⌘+ is really ⌘= on a US layout, so bind both `=` and `+`, as every browser does. **(d) Accessibility is the real reason this matters, and it argues for scope.** macOS Dynamic Type does not reach an AppKit/SwiftUI desktop app the way it does on iOS, so a fixed 11 pt `captionFont` is simply small on a 5K display with no system-level remedy — the app is the only place that can fix it. Which also means the setting belongs in Settings as a slider beside the appearance control, not only behind a keystroke. **Rails:** no backend involvement (this is `@AppStorage`, per-machine, never a bank file); the persisted value survives relaunch; `Actual Size` (⌘0) always returns to 1.0; scaled layouts must not clip — the existing `fixedSize(horizontal: false, vertical: true)` habit in the status/hint views is the pattern; and the sidebar's ⌘1–6 must keep working at every scale. → extends **G107** (the mascot renders at fixed pt sizes and would need to pick a sprite scale), **G124** (the Sources grid is the densest surface and the first place a scale bug shows), the PR #49 theme-store work (same observation mechanism, same idempotent-setter trap); relates **G109** (never reheat or re-fit the graph as a side effect of a chrome change). APPLY, S–M — **slice 1** = the scale store + derived theme constants + the View menu commands + the Settings slider; **slice 2** (only if measured as needed) = the graph web view's `pageZoom`. **Slice 1a shipped (2026-09-05, PR #54)**: `ThemeStore.uiScale` (clamped `0.8...1.4`, stepped by `0.1`, `cicada.uiScale`), the five font + six spacing tokens now computed off it, `CicadaTheme.font(size:weight:design:)`, `zoomIn`/`zoomOut`/`resetZoom`, `BookwormRenderer.snappedPointSize` (mascot stays on 24 px cells), a View menu (`CommandGroup(after: .sidebar)`) for ⌘=/⌘−/⌘0 plus a local key monitor for ⌘⇧=, and a new Settings *General* tab (Appearance + a Text size slider). **Slice 1b remains open** — the mechanical `.system(size:)` → `CicadaTheme.font(size:)` migration across ~46 files plus the source-lint that keeps literals out, its own follow-up PR. **Slice 2** (graph `pageZoom`) still open on a measured need. **Follow-up noted, not fixed:** the 96 literal-width `.frame(width:)` sites are untouched per R7 — any that visibly clip at the 1.4 cap on the demo bank is a follow-up line here, not a reason to touch them now. | 🛠️ slice 1a shipped; slice 1b + slice 2 open |
| G131 | **Cicada should replace the harness's own auto-memory — research whether we can do it better, and what it would take** (Rodrigo 2026-09-04: "as part of a backlog do you think maybe we can replace the auto memory feature with cicada? … document the idea of researching if we can implement our own version of auto memory that is better for cicada") | **What the competitor actually is, stated precisely, because the shape is uncomfortably close to ours.** Claude Code keeps a per-project memory at `~/.claude/projects/<sanitised-cwd>/memory/`: one markdown file per fact, YAML frontmatter (`name`, `description`, `metadata.type` ∈ user | feedback | project | reference), `[[wikilink]]`s between files, and a `MEMORY.md` index whose one-line-per-fact list is injected into context at **every** session start. That is a markdown knowledge graph with an always-loaded cursor into it — i.e. **`entities/` plus `_state.md`, built by someone else, sitting next to the bank and invisible to it.** On this machine it measured 36.8 KB / ~9.2k est. tokens before a manual prune. **The case that Cicada wins on merit, not on ownership — four things it already has that auto-memory structurally lacks:** **(1) Decay.** Auto-memory never ages. Nothing marks a fact stale, nothing notices it was superseded, and the only remedy is a human re-reading the directory — which is exactly what happened on 2026-09-04, when four of thirteen files (a July audit, a shipped design assessment, a pause-point its own index line called superseded) had to be hand-deleted after months of being loaded into every session as if current. G66 solves this by construction, and this is the sharpest argument in the row: **a memory system with no clock is a memory system that lies with increasing confidence.** **(2) Provenance.** An auto-memory file records `originSessionId` and nothing else — no author, no episode, no evidence span, no git history. Cicada has `Cicada-Author:`/`Cicada-Session:` trailers, `source_episodes`, and G118 spans, so a belief can be traced to the sentence that produced it. **(3) Contradiction handling.** Auto-memory's instruction is "check for an existing file that already covers it" — a prose convention, enforced by nothing. Cicada has the claim layer, bi-temporal validity and the inbox: a competing value opens a question instead of quietly creating a second file. **(4) It is one harness's private store.** It does not exist for Codex, it does not travel between machines, and it is not the person's to take with them — which is the portability point the whole project is built on. **What auto-memory has that we do not, and it is the only thing that matters: zero-cost injection.** Its index is in context *before the first token*, with no tool call and no latency, so the agent starts already knowing. Cicada's recall costs a round trip and depends on the model choosing to make it — the G105 objection, applied to reading rather than writing. **The good news is the port is largely built.** G75's handshake is already delivered through MCP `initialize`'s `instructions` field — the same pre-first-token slot — and already carries a now-view from `_state.md` under a token budget. So the question this row exists to answer is **not** "how do we inject memory at session start" (solved) but **"what belongs in that slot besides the now-view?"** Concretely: auto-memory's `feedback` type — *how to work with this person on this project* — is the one category the handshake carries nothing of, and it is precisely WikiSkill's third layer, i.e. **G112**. A `skill`-type entity is the natural home; the research is whether Sleep can distil those reliably from episodes, or whether they must stay user-asserted. **Open questions the row exists to answer, cheapest first:** (a) Can a `SessionStart` hook write the handshake into context for harnesses that drop MCP `instructions`? `handshake.HOOK_POINTER` already anticipates this and the hook itself is G49/G76 — measure whether that path alone closes the gap. (b) What is the honest token budget for a lessons block on top of the ≤ 1,800-token primer, and what gets trimmed first (the `_state.md` trim order is the precedent: people → preferences → conversations → projects). (c) Does a `feedback` belief decay like any other, or is it `durable`? A working agreement ("PRs go to dev") should not fade; an observation about a paused queue should. (d) Migration: is a one-time import of the existing auto-memory directory worth it, or is it cleaner to let the two coexist until Cicada's version is demonstrably better? **Rails.** **Cicada must never read the harness's memory directory uninvited** — it sits beside `~/.claude/`'s transcripts and falls under the same rule that keeps those unread (G105/G93); an import is a user-initiated action with a visible preview, exactly like every other export path, never a background sweep. Nothing here creates a second store: a migrated fact becomes an ordinary entity or claim in the bank, with provenance saying it came from a harness memory file. And the win is only real if it is **portable** — a lessons block that works in Claude Code and nowhere else has reimplemented the thing it was meant to replace. → extends **G75** (the handshake is the delivery slot), **G112** (compiling experience into loadable skills is the same layer), **G53** (`_state.md` is the existing always-loaded cursor); consumes **G66** (decay is the differentiator), **G118** (provenance is the other one); relates **G49**/**G76** (the SessionStart hook), **G117** (onboarding is where a person would opt into this). RESEARCH, M — measure (a) and (b) before writing any code. | 🔲 |

## Open decisions (asked to Rodrigo — answers recorded here)

These are the foundational forks; most of the backlog hangs off them.

- **D1 — Storage backend.** Stay markdown+git+LEANN? Move toward Postgres+pgvector (Honcho/gbrain)? Hybrid (markdown = source of truth, pg = index)? — _awaiting_
- **D2 — Entity model philosophy.** Keep closed 8-type set + promotion gate? Move toward Honcho-style emergent/belief/observer-observed (drop promotion)? Hybrid (entities + per-context dimensions)? — _awaiting_
- **D3 — Retrieval interface.** Add a natural-language `ask`/dialectic endpoint (agent queries memory in NL)? Keep direct file traversal? Both? — _awaiting_
- **D4 — Peers & multi-bank scope.** Build peers (humans/agents/robots equal) + multiple memory banks as a near-term feature, or research-only for now? — _awaiting_

> Answers (2026-06-16):
>
> - **D1 (storage): DECIDED (2026-06-17)** — markdown+git stays the source of truth; **add a derived embedding index, and Rodrigo is willing to go straight to Postgres+pgvector** (rather than sqlite-vec first) so pgvector + derived indexes land directly, then the ask endpoint. Research recommended sqlite-vec-first for a single-user bundle; the Postgres-direct path is viable because the index is *derived/rebuildable* — see dossier §D1 for the tradeoff. LEANN is being replaced either way.
> - **D2 (entity model): research-only** — no commitment yet; R4 + R7 findings inform it. Keep closed types + promotion for now.
> - **D3 (retrieval): BOTH** ✅ — add a natural-language `ask`/dialectic endpoint (answer + git-blame citations + gap analysis) AND keep direct file traversal. → unblocks A5; new design item.
> - **D4 (peers + multi-bank): research-only** — design the peer (observer/observed) model + multi-bank "memory projects", don't build yet. R8 informs it.

**Consequence of D3 = BOTH:** the `ask`/dialectic endpoint is now a committed design item
(not just research). It folds in A5 (gap analysis) and the Honcho/gbrain "answer not pages"
insight. Spec to be written once R1/R7 land. Everything else stays research-gated.

### Reference prompts (G77/G78/G79 — the voice-agent + gbrain + north-star directive)

Rodrigo's raw directive of 2026-09-01 (Claude Code session `1d742a99-90a0-46a2-a0d9-4642052335bf`),
preserved verbatim as the second prompt-library entry. It forwards a public build log by
[@KingBootoshi](https://x.com/KingBootoshi) (quoted inside it) and produced G77, G78 and G79:

> BOOTOSHI 👑
> @KingBootoshi
> I'm working on a very interesting voice agent problem I'd like to share with ya'll -
>
> First, my desire: I want to speak to ONE single consistent agent who handles EVERYTHING for me - this includes keeping track of (the present) + keeping track of projects + delegating work
>
> Stack shown in the image, but I'm basically using Hermes as the agent daemon + custom voice pipeline via Pipecat
>
> Here's the biggest problem: for the voice model to be an intuitive UX (as if I'm speaking to another human) the voice model has to be fast, which means it needs to be small, which means it can't be SUPER intelligent
>
> Now is the small voice model being not-so intelligent a problem? i have to see what PhoneLLM is capable of but my first thought is "yes it is"
>
> The idea is I talk to this voice agent (not so intelligent) who delegates tasks to Hermes agents (which are intelligent, but take a long time)
>
> We want to make sure that our direction we give via voice is NOT lost in translation by the voice model to Hermes - and vice versa. We MUST make sure what is being said by the Hermes agents is being properly vocalized to us, with nothing important lost in translation
>
> GPT-Live solves half of this problem of a fast UX by having the voice agent delegate to Codex agents. Unfortunately we do not have that latest ChatGPT tech, so what can the layman with hardware do?
>
> My current idea is UTILIZING the intelligence of Hermes agents in order to create OUTPUT packets in a conversational manner
>
> Instead of trying to squeeze intelligence and reliability out of the small voice model, we will instead force the output of the intelligent (Hermes) agents to speak in a way the voice agent can make NO mistakes in processing
>
> I actually imagined this voice agent being in a room with a green light that turns on and off
>
> Anytime a Hermes agent finishes work, it outputs a packet of everything we need to know, written in a way an absolute dumbass can understand
>
> Then the voice agent does not need to try to understand technical complex jargon, it just reads the packet to us, and inputs our response back
>
> It's quite a simple solution and I'm excited to implement and try this!
>
> I've gotten the Facetime adapter (that I open-sourced) working well and a daemon running on my mini that answers only calls from MY phone
>
> On the side I am working on a neural net that ONLY recognizes my voice to act as a form of voice authentication when I make the call (it does a great job at filtering background noise too)
>
> By FAR this is one of my favorite builds I've ever attempted, because when I succeed it should accelerate my general workflow 10x
>
> add this as inspiration of things to discuss as a to-do in the backend as there is some stuff here i want to implement. Also add to backlog to search if gbain's approach is actually better for what we try to do, or if there is something he does better that maybe we can learn from. https://github.com/garrytan/gbrain . Important also is to check https://github.com/garrytan/gbrain-evals so as to see if any of that can apply for us. and add to the backlog this thought that the best version of cicada is one that smartly gives and learns from all my experience as a human + the things that i've curated and might be relevant to me.

### Reference prompt (G96 — Ed Honour on vector search vs. the rest of the stack)

Forwarded by Rodrigo 2026-09-01, transcribed from a reel in his feed, preserved verbatim as the
source for G96:

> So vector databases and vector search are an important part of memory systems, but they are by
> far not the most important part. And the reason for this is a vector search is non-deterministic.
> It is very possible that when you do a vector search against your database, you are not going to
> return all of the records or the most important records that you're looking for. So what you want
> to do in a memory system is combine a vector search and a knowledge graph. So the vector search is
> the entry way into the knowledge graph, and then once you're in the knowledge graph, you traverse
> the knowledge graph. Now that is a really important concept, but it is not the only important
> concept. You can also find your way into a knowledge graph through relational databases. So a
> relational database may query a record, and then that is going to drive the traversal of the
> knowledge graph based on the record that you queried. So vector databases are important, but they
> are definitely not the only thing. And if I needed to leave one part out of my memory system, it
> would actually be the vector database. You can't get rid of the relational database, you can't get
> rid of the document database, and you can't get rid of the knowledge graph, but the vector
> database is not necessarily the most important thing, even though because it's new, most people
> focus on it.
